  Hello Adam and everybody. :-) Thank you for your next, great lesson, Adam. As about mineral water: I don't know how it is in Canada, but here in Poland, we don't call artificially mineralised water "mineral water", and I don't think that anybody do such thing here at all. We have "Natural mineral water" /various types of mineral waters, actually/, and it is from natural sources only. If not, you are not allowed to label it this way - then it is called "drinking water", or "spring water", or something like that. One can add some effervescence tablets to their glass of tap water, naturally ... and some people have mineral water in their faucets/taps on an everyday basis - it depends on what source their water comes from. ;-D  Greetings from Warsaw, Poland :-)

  Very clear explanation, a great video, thank you! :-)
  
  I would like to ask English native speakers for one specific phrase. When I am in a car with someone who is driving, and I sit in the passenger seat, often times there is the situation when we come to the crossroad and we need to give the right of the way to the cars coming from the left as well as from the right. I help the driver to pay attention to the cars, so I am checking the road coming from the right side (closer to me) and tell the driver if there is a car coming. What phrase do you use in English, when there is no car and we are free to go? In Czech we say "zprava dobrý", which literally translates as "good from the right" = it is good to go, there is no car coming from the right. But I have no idea what to use when the driver speaks only English. Is there a short phrase for that, please? :)

      Hema Singh 👨🏻‍🏫 Hello, Hema. Mr Teacher here in England 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿. I have an answer to your question. We use ‘throughout’  to mean from the start to the end or continuously. An example here is - Throughout this lesson, Adam talked about cars.’ We use ‘through’ to mean passing along or going in a certain direction. An example here is - ‘I walked through a tunnel to reach the railway station.’ ‘Walking through the forest was very pleasant. I heard some birds singing.’ It can refer to time. An example here is - ‘Through the many years of his life the old man never stayed in hospital.’

      Kiara Valle Montalbán 👨🏻‍🏫 Hello, Kiara. Mr Teacher here in England 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿. I have an answer to your question about ‘sorry’. We use it to offer an apology to someone, or to be sympathetic towards someone in distress. Here are some examples for you.
      ‘I am so sorry to hear about the death of your father.’
      ‘Sorry for keeping you waiting.’
      ‘I’m sorry to say that I have lost your book.’
      ‘I’m sorry but I cannot come to your party as I will be on holiday.’

  Hello Adam! I would like to know how to use "move along and move forward" please 🙏
    ↳ **@sirmeowthelibrarycat** (👍0 · 2021-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a5nFBQ5H62U&lc=Ugxf8RYqt6uqmS8IH3x4AaABAg.97lJPpCG7vy9DemigBJMeZ
      Brenda Noemi mendez niz 👨🏻‍🏫 Hello, Brenda. Mr Teacher here in England 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿. I have an answer to your question. 
      We use ‘move along’ to tell people to leave where they are. It happens when officials or the police try to disperse a crowd such as at a public meeting or sports event where bad behaviour could occur.
      We use ‘move forward’ to indicate the direction someone should take. We move forward in a queue (line) at the post office.

If you are writing fiction, then both formal and colloquial English are acceptable, because you are creating your own context for writing.

    ↳ **@sirmeowthelibrarycat** (👍0 · 2021-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a5nFBQ5H62U&lc=Ugwzxh4Yp3tl3HGGcMF4AaABAg.97j6LLbKnIB9Der5M2AEGq
      Viktor Nazar 👨🏻‍🏫 Hello, Viktor. Mr Teacher here in England 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿. I have an answer for you. The statement you quote is not a valid sentence, but a contraction that becomes a headline in a magazine or newspaper article. In full it is like this - ‘Volunteers are to be infected with corona virus for £XXX in (a) bid (to) find (a) vaccine.’ The number of volunteers would also be mentioned to give the experiment scientific validity.

  I having a question for a long time. 
  No one can give me the answer yet.  It bothers me a lot.
  
  
  1. I saw him cross the street
  2. he was seen to cross the street.
  3. I saw him crossing the street.
  4. he was seen crossing the street.
  
  
  they are all correct， and I can understand 【3】,【4】.
  but for 【1】 and【2】.
  I know that it’s incorrect if 【1】 become 【I saw he ”to“ cross the street】, but why？ 
  If I do this. What does this sentence mean?
  
  
  I also know【2】 must have 【to】，but why this【to】is necessary?
  If it is taken away. How do native speakers feel?
  
  
  I want to know the underlying reason that why the infinitive with Passive Voice of Perceptive Verbs must have 【to】。
  
  
  Thank you very much !
    ↳ **@sirmeowthelibrarycat** (👍0 · 2021-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a5nFBQ5H62U&lc=Ugz6u-vIPucaV5aGTnx4AaABAg.983h95BHyy_9DesmdrRm0l
      周欣荣 👨🏻‍🏫 Hello. Mr Teacher here in England 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿. I have just found your question. It is an advanced level problem for learner s.Perhaps I can help you understand it. There are two points to discuss. One concerns pronouns and the other concerns verb tenses. When they mix together you have your problem. In example one, you are using the third person masculine pronoun and the simple past tense with the direct voice. You are the observer of what the man did. However, in example two you are telling me about something using the third person masculine pronoun and the past perfect tense, which has to use ‘to’. Here you are using the indirect voice as a reporter of what the man did. I hope this is helpful for you.

**@privateerburrows** (👍7 · 2020-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RX3bwncQI7M&lc=UgwS5UpqLHBPPQ54ws94AaABAg
  Sorry to be pedantic but...
  A) "beans" is a misnomer for coffee. The coffee SEEDS may look like beans but they are seeds. You don't "pick the beans"; you pick the frits and extract the seeds.
  B) Fair Trade is very poorly defined here. To say "locals are not exploited" is inaccurate, as human resources are always exploited when when workers are hired or contracted. There is nothing negative about "exploitation", per-se;  a more apt term would be "unfairly exploited" as in using slave labor or child labor.  Fair Trade also usually implies lower environmental impact and less multinational ownership structures involved.
  C) "long" meaning extra shot is entirely incorrect;  "long" means extra WATER. Extra long would mean Americano.
  The number of shots (amount of coffee used) is orthogonal to the amount of water used. You can have single short, lingle long, double short or double long.
  D) "Body", in wine, means how thick or viscous the wine is, but is typically increased by adding glycerine to the wine, which has the added benefit of sweetening the wine.  Glycerine is a "wood alcohol" in the same family of sugar substitutes as xilitol and erythritol. I have no idea what "body" means in the case of coffee;  probably nothing at all.

  Which of the two sentences is correct? 'What did you do' or 'what you did'? 
  
  
  
  
  and why
    ↳ **@sirmeowthelibrarycat** (👍0 · 2021-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nEx49LO-j-c&lc=UgzjqXbxIV3Bf7tmtvd4AaABAg.96RS2cqhDcr9Dnypv8KR_c
      Winston Alejandro Güichardo Pérez 👨🏻‍🏫 Hello, Winston. Mr Teacher here in England 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿. I have an answer to your question.
      Example sentence One is correct but it needs a question mark to complete it. Example sentence Two is incorrect. Something is missing from it after ‘did’. This is how it should look: ‘What you did is unforgivable’ or ‘What you did was very brave of you. Rescuing my cat from a fierce dog was dangerous.’

  Dear Adam, Your lessons helped me a lot and has improved my English. Can you please let me know about Precis writing in detail. How to write a Precis?
    ↳ **@sirmeowthelibrarycat** (👍1 · 2021-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EZpSew68-eI&lc=UgxQ_AqRNy4AuslbOSR4AaABAg.97BqqJTclgX9Djrr3PH4Dj
      Muhammad Faisal Irshad 👨🏻‍🏫 Hello, Muhammad. Mr Teacher here in England 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿. I can help you with your question. 
      A précis is a short version of a text. Start by reading the text at least twice (2 times). Next, look for the nouns in each paragraph and make a list of them. Then decide what the meaning of the paragraph is using those nouns and their associated descriptors (pronouns, adjectives). Now you can draft the first version of the précis in the third person. Finally, write it once more with changes to correct the grammar if needed. The précis you have written should look and read like a mini essay.

  how to use had,has,and have? thanks
    ↳ **@sirmeowthelibrarycat** (👍0 · 2021-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EZpSew68-eI&lc=UgxOPVxkMUKEURn5SOl4AaABAg.98_2Cua3A8i9DjtAcDrr1J
      NEVER STOP LEARNING 👨🏻‍🏫 Hello. Mr Teacher here in England 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿. I can help you with your question.
      These words are part of the verb ‘to be’ or ‘to have’. They refer to both time and person. Examples are
      : ‘I have a beautiful cat.’
      This is the first person neuter pronoun and the present tense.
      Adam tells you that ‘He has a beautiful cat.’
      This is the third person masculine pronoun in the present tense.
      Adam tells that ‘He had a beautiful cat.’ 
      This is the same as the second sentence except that is in the Past simple tense.

  I don't know when to use "orange juice" or "juice of orange". A few days ago I was watching a movie when I saw "Department of justice" and I thought "Why not Justice Department"? I'm confused.
    ↳ **@sirmeowthelibrarycat** (👍0 · 2021-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EZpSew68-eI&lc=UgzjhF-pIsJM8TjWiYZ4AaABAg.97F_PlqItIG9DjuCirchi-
      Camilo Andrés Ramírez Pernía 👨🏻‍🏫 Hell, Camilo. Mr Teacher here in England 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿. I can help you with your question. The difference between these examples is not that obvious. What we can say is that ‘something of something’ is standard English with specific application. Your example ‘Department of Justice’ is like that. It is possible to use ‘Justice Department’ more in spoken English, as when a news reporter is on camera. We do not use your other example very much as it places the words in an awkward sequence. ‘Orange juice’ is the standard form, not ‘juice of orange’.

  Part of speech -  every word in English has a part of speech ( category) subject verb ect 
  Noun adjective verb adverb pronounce article conjunction preposition and interjection 
  
  Nouns and adjectives - 
  Person place thing 
  
  What is a person ( a person name is a proper noun and it takes a capital letter ) example Bill 
  Title - CEO Student 
  Age Ect 
  
  Place 
  Name - Paris 
  Location - East 
  
  Thing - animate   and inanimate ( not alive ) street ect ( can be seen by the five senses) both of them 
  
  Abstract  = Ideas , concept, feeling, imaginary - cannot see and touch it for example happiness ....concept ... Happiness ....money  ( it don't exist you have paper and metal  money is the idea of value added to these things ) 
  Imaginary - unicorn  subject ( math is just an idea cannot see and touch it ) activity - swimming ( it's still a thing ) 
  
  Using nouns -
  
  Pure and simple noun - a ball you can catch it and hold it 
  Gerund ( ING noun ) - they are verbs that are changed into noun it is not pure  ( an action then convert into a thing  example  smoking ... to smoke( verb ) and smoking is the activity there is no simple and pure noun for that activity

  I want to ask a question
  You said "every sentence has a noun", but what if I have this sentence "write"?
  Which is instructive sentence, is it incorrect sentence depending on what you said?
    ↳ **@raistlinov** (👍1 · 2020-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EZpSew68-eI&lc=Ugxsl5cLiTED_dQjzVt4AaABAg.96ul0DXcMAR97GOsUSLrcm
      Simple directive sentences like "Write.", "Run.", and "Go!" all have an implied subject. The implied subject is the person to whom the directive is, well, directed. 
      
      For example, you're telling Bob to write but Bob already knows you're addressing him so instead of saying, "Bob, write!" you simply say, "Write!" There is still the implied subject, "Bob".

      Mohamed Mifras the first one is incorrect because worry is a verb there, so we cannot use a verb where there is this verb 'to be', we see 'am' in that sentence. **@rentiap** (👍1 · 2020-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=phEwlgjVUWQ&lc=UgxamLlT9Wk8uZnXZp94AaABAg
  Greetings Adam:
   I have been reading through many of the titles of your posts and most if not all of them seem to have parts in the headers written in a language that I was never taught in English classes throughout my years of grammar school and high school. @ California.
  
   Many of the headers or Titles, and parts within the videos are written in all caps, of which I cannot find in either of the styles manuals that I have copies of >> The New oxford style manual and the Chicago manual of style 16th edition.
  
  
   The only thing I have found that comes close is what's known as "American SIGN language" @ page 576 section 11.147 under foreign languages, Glosses in ASL. "The written-language transcription of a sign is called a gloss. 
  
  Glosses are words from the spoken language written in small capital letters: WOMAN, SCHOOL, CAT. (Alternatively, regular capital letters may be used.) When two or more written words are used to gloss a single sign, the glosses are separated by hyphens. The translation is enclosed in double quotation marks. The sign for "a car drove by" is written as VEHICLE-DRIVE-BY.
  * One obvious limitation of the use of glosses from the spoken/written language to present signs is that there are no one-to-one correspondence between the words or signs in any two languages."*  But; 
  You are not including the hyphens.
  
  
   I have not been able to find the style that you are using.
   Which styles manuals are you referring to when you are writing the titles,headers or within the texts of  your videos?
  
   When I attended school I was taught to correctly write my name with the first letter of each word capitalized, and never was it to be written in all caps. [That was only to be used on a ledger or on a tomb stone signifying a dead "person".] (of course we know that legally a "person" never includes man or woman.)
   Or, when using all caps, each letter is the first letter of an abbreviation.
   As an example. > BIBLE could be the symbol or sign for -> Basic Instructions Before Leaving Earth.
   SYMBOLIC TEXT LIKE THIS [JOHN PAUL JONES] cannot grammatically constitute (in proper English)a word or a name in any way.
   In parts the United States of America in the 1960's grammar and Latin were removed from the school curriculum. Latin is used in symbolic text, and grammar is the science of written language.
   Without such skills, you will never know the difference between what grammatically constitutes a name and what does not constitute a name, landing you into a state of confusion. I look forward to hearing from you. Thank you, Geod.

  Can u plz tell me the diff between underated or overated diff with example??
    ↳ **@WorldEnglishTeacher** (👍0 · 2020-08-13) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=phEwlgjVUWQ&lc=Ugws9DoE9TXzw88gUYJ4AaABAg.91mzkjffYRe923OWP3SsGU
      Underrated is when something is considered less capable or valuable than it is in reality. For example: "Gary Oldman is an underrated actor."
      
      Overrated is basically the opposite. It's when something is considered better or more valuable than it is in reality. For example: "Messi is good, but I think he's overrated."

  John 'went' to Brazil is the same as 'John has gone Brazil'
  
  These are the tenses that I find very unnecessary in English 
  
  Where have you gone? = where are you?
  Where have you been? = Where did you go?
  
  "Where have you been?" 
  "I've been to  a lot of places"
  
  Using the word 'already' does alter this.
